#5184. Millions watched Kalshi’s election odds without betting a dollar

On election night, millions watched Kalshi's probabilities shift in real time, even though most visitors never traded a contract.

Kalshi says people watching its midterm markets outnumbered traders five to one. For those spectators, Kalshi offers a forecast that keeps moving while the country waits for an answer.

You could spend election night on Kalshi without buying anything, as millions of people did this week. Its midterm display offered maps of congressional races and gauges tracking control of the House and Senate, with percentages changing in real time as the evening unfolded.

It's easy to understand why traders keep such a close eye on Kalshi: every little change in odds changes the value of their positions. Given how much money actually moved hands during the midterms, it's no wonder hundreds of thousands of traders were glued to the platforms.

But millions of people were also looking at Kalshi who weren't making any bets, with most not even having accounts on the platform. In October, Kalshi said viewers of its midterm markets outnumbered traders five to one.

Kalshi’s post-election recap reported more than $2.5 billion in midterm trading volume, compared with $1.3 billion for the 2024 election cycle.

Kalshi's Nov. 4, 2026 recap reports $1.3 billion for the 2024 election cycle and more than $2.5 billion for 2026 midterm markets. The 2026 bar marks the reported lower-bound threshold, not an exact total. Kalshi uses notional volume to measure trading activity; these figures do not count viewers or unique traders. The recap does not specify matching market coverage or start and end times for the two periods.

Source: Kalshi: 2026 midterms recap, Nov. 4, 2026 · Kalshi: prediction-market volume convention, footnote 1

While Kalshi's numbers are hard to verify and the company hasn't provided an exact measurement period or a definition of its ratio, they show a second use for the exchange: following politics without placing a bet, while letting the people who do bet help shape your expectations.

On Aug. 20, 2008, Wolf Blitzer turned to CNN's internet reporter, Abbi Tatton, for an update on speculation about Barack Obama's running mate. Tatton brought him a number from Intrade, an online prediction market: Joe Biden was trading at about 36%.

Biden led the other candidates she named and would ultimately get the job, although 36% still left substantially more room for someone else to be chosen.

Tatton also mentioned CNN's own political market, which used virtual dollars. Viewers could follow the odds or play along without risking real money. Everybody wanted to put a number on political suspense, and it was a perfect fit for a television segment about the elections.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Kalshi's election hub combines market odds with polling averages, fundraising figures, and campaign news, giving visitors several ways to follow a race in one place.

A contract paying $1 if a candidate wins, and nothing if they lose, might trade at 65 cents. That price is commonly read as roughly a 65% chance of victory, although fees, available trading volume, and the contract's rules slightly erode those numbers.

Polls gather answers from a sample of people, forecasts estimate an outcome based on historical evidence and assumptions, and a market then sets a price at which participants will trade. These can inform one another, but a percentage alone doesn't reveal how it was produced or what it measures.

The trader has to decide whether 65 cents is worth paying, and the spectator can simply take away the expectation that the candidate will probably win. That means that someone else's financial judgment has become part of their understanding of the election.

The market still has something to show you between a poll's release and the next substantial development. It can adjust after a debate, absorb new returns, or reflect a change in how traders interpret already available evidence.

Those movements look much the same on a screen: a percentage rises or falls, inviting the viewer to read the change as news.

Kalshi acknowledges in its explanation of why prices move that fresh information is only one cause. Trader behavior can also push prices away from a reasonable estimate, and watching the percentage doesn't necessarily reveal what has happened.

A candidate can appear to be gaining ground because the chance attached to their name has increased, even when no additional voter has been interviewed and no additional ballot counted.

An experiment involving 1,994 registered voters showed how easily those percentages can shape expectations.

Participants saw pairs of hypothetical congressional candidates, with randomly varied polling figures and market odds. Higher displayed odds made respondents more likely to identify a candidate as the expected winner.

Traders can bring knowledge that a casual observer lacks, and money gives someone who spots a mistaken price a reason to act. The commercial effort to attract an audience doesn't remove the possibility that the resulting forecast is good.

But a poll and a market reacting to that poll can look like two separate endorsements of a candidate's prospects when they draw on the same evidence. Those just observing the market get more perspectives without necessarily getting more independent information.

In its post-election recap, Kalshi said its markets reached 99% for Democratic House control at 8:10 p.m. Eastern and passed that level for Senate control at 9 p.m.

The company said its prices reflected anticipated results ahead of television networks and offered replays showing how the graphics changed through the evening.

But an exchange and a newsroom have to answer different questions, since a market can express overwhelming confidence while leaving room for a loss.

The Associated Press says it calls a race only when the trailing candidates no longer have a path to victory. For congressional control, it counts the seats actually needed for a majority.

Neither a price nor a news organization's call is the official certification of an election. But the call takes responsibility for saying the outcome has been established, a different claim from assigning it a probability.

A market that gets to 99% first doesn't show that journalists took longer to perform the same task.

AP supplies Kalshi with vote counts and race calls, which the exchange places alongside its forecasts and traders can use to reconsider their positions. The market depends in part on the reporting against which it is judged.

Kalshi's recap points readers toward international elections and the 2028 presidential contest, inviting them to follow the odds beyond one election night.

Spectators will most likely want an early estimate from a prediction market but still wait for a newsroom to declare a winner.

The price of these contracts shows how strongly traders expect an outcome, and the race call says the remaining votes can no longer change it. People who never risk a dollar still have to decide how much authority to give the price, especially when its movement is the only thing that has changed.

Armed with a classical education and an eye for news, Andjela dove head deep into the crypto industry in 2018 after spending years covering politics.

#5183. Binance Hackers Swap About $50 Million of Stolen XRP Mostly Into Bitcoin, Elliptic Says

Hackers tied to the record Binance theft have begun converting a large slice of the stolen XRP into bitcoin, blockchain analytics firm Elliptic said in an update dated through Friday, with about 42 million XRP — roughly $50 million — already swapped, mostly through THORChain.

Converted bitcoin from other stolen assets nearly doubled overnight to about 1,650 BTC, worth some $124 million, Elliptic wrote. Most of that jump came from the XRP route. About 192 million XRP, roughly $226 million, remained unswapped in addresses linked to the Binance theft.

The XRP was part of the Oct. 29 drain that Binance later put at $6.34 billion. Between 20:05 and 20:09 UTC that day, five hot wallets sent 233.46 million XRP to an attacker address. The FBI has attributed the campaign to North Korea’s TraderTraitor group. Binance has reopened withdrawals in stages and said it has frozen or intercepted about $2.85 billion while covering customer losses from its own funds.

The stolen XRP sat quiet at first. Early on Nov. 2, between 02:12 and 04:00 UTC, all about 233 million XRP moved into six new addresses, where it stayed until Nov. 5, Elliptic said. From the afternoon of Nov. 5, two of those six addresses began sending XRP on to dozens of new accounts that swapped it, mostly through THORChain into bitcoin.

A small amount of ether also went through THORChain into bitcoin that evening. The main bitcoin and ether holdings from the theft — the largest pools — have still not moved, Elliptic said. That pattern matches what the firm saw after the Bybit hack: turn freezable and mid-liquidity tokens into bitcoin first, then attempt cash-out later through OTC brokers.

The broader laundering trail is already vast. Since the Binance theft, Elliptic has counted more than 20,000 wallets on 16 blockchains, at least 36 services and at least 65,000 transactions — roughly one new wallet every 30 seconds and one transaction every 10 seconds. About $3.11 billion remains in addresses linked to the Binance theft, mostly as ether and bitcoin. A negligible amount has likely been cashed out so far.

Issuers can freeze many stablecoins. They cannot freeze XRP the same way, and they cannot freeze bitcoin. After Tether and Circle locked down large stablecoin tranches on Oct. 29, the attacker prioritized assets that sit outside that kill switch. Swapping XRP into bitcoin continues that logic: bitcoin is harder to claw back once it is fragmented across fresh addresses.

All of the stolen SOL, about $87 million at Elliptic’s prices, has already been swapped, bridged or mixed, with much of it ending as bitcoin. Most early Tron laundering — about $220 million in TRX — has also run, a flow Bitcoin Magazine noted when covering the quiet shutdown of the T3 Financial Crime Unit before the hack. Only small parts of the main ether and NEAR piles have moved.

U.S. bank supervisors have already checked in with lenders after the Binance breach. For bitcoin markets, the nearer question is how much more of the remaining ~192 million XRP — and eventually the parked ether — gets turned into BTC before anyone tries to cash out.

The test now is whether THORChain operators and other bridges keep blocking the published attacker addresses fast enough to slow the XRP-to-bitcoin pipe, or whether the rest of that pile follows the same overnight path the first $50 million already took.

#5182. Morpho loan stress exposes cbXRP, but spares native XRP

A Nov. 6 snapshot placed 8.84 million USDC across 1,070 positions within a further 10% oracle decline, assuming unchanged debt and collateral.

A 10% decline from the collateral price recorded around 13:24 UTC on Nov. 6 would make positions carrying about 8.84 million USDC of debt eligible for liquidation in a Morpho lending market on Base.

The snapshot covered 1,070 positions borrowing USDC against cbXRP, Coinbase's XRP-backed token. A smaller 5% decline would bring 462 of those positions, carrying about 3.23 million USDC of debt, to or beyond the same boundary.

For XRP holders who borrow against their tokens, further price weakness creates a choice between repaying debt, committing more collateral, or facing liquidation. Borrowing preserves exposure to the asset, while adding a financial obligation that becomes harder to support as collateral loses value.

Coinbase describes cbXRP as a wrapped token backed 1:1 by native XRP held in its custody. A holder can use an existing XRP position as collateral, so rising loan balances alone do not create new XRP purchases.

At 13:24 UTC, the market carried about 59.2 million USDC of total debt against roughly 119.9 million cbXRP in collateral. The debt inside the further-10% stress bucket represented approximately 15% of that borrowing, and the affected positions held about 12.73 million cbXRP.

Conditional exposure in one Base cbXRP/USDC market, using November 6 positions with debt and collateral quantities held fixed.

An illustrative 60% starting LTV rises to 66.7% when collateral value falls 10%, leaving debt and collateral quantities unchanged.

At the same lower oracle price, repaying 10% of original debt restores the illustrative position to 60% LTV.

Adding about 11.1% to original collateral quantity at the lower oracle price restores 60% LTV.

The 5% group sits inside the 10% group. Repayments or added collateral can change the outcome.

November 6, 2026 13:24 UTC API state timestamps. Paginated requests span multiple observations. Fixed-position scenarios establish conditional eligibility. Actual seizures and subsequent native-XRP sales require separate transactions. Borrower example is hypothetical and excludes interest, fees and further price changes.

Public GraphQL POST, three pages of 500/500/70 positions. Include health factor at or below 1/(1-d). Six decimals per token. Bars share a linear 0–10m USDC scale and use unrounded debt. Values shown to 2 decimals in millions. Illustration starts at 60% LTV. After a 10% oracle fall, 60/0.9=66.7%; repaying 10% of debt or adding 1/0.9-1=11.1% collateral restores 60%.

Its liquidation loan-to-value threshold is fixed at 62.5%. As the collateral price falls, the same debt takes up a larger share of the backing, bringing the position closer to liquidation eligibility.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The market's oracle, the price feed used to value collateral, uses Chainlink's XRP/USD feed with a fixed 1:1 USDC leg, and its reported ratio was about 1.1843 USDC per cbXRP.

Borrower actions can change the exposed balance before prices reach either scenario.

Consider a position starting at 60% LTV. A 10% fall in the oracle price, with debt and collateral quantities unchanged, raises its LTV to about 66.7%, beyond this market's 62.5% threshold.

Repaying 10% of the debt would restore the original 60% LTV at the lower price. Adding about 11.1% to the original collateral quantity would achieve the same result, excluding interest, fees, and further price moves.

The market interface recorded repayments and collateral additions around 13:14-13:16 UTC on Nov. 6. Those transactions show that positions were changing around the observation window.

For borrowers using Coinbase's interface to Morpho on Base, optional automatic collateral protection can add assets after a configured trigger. Borrowers must enable it and have assets available, and volatility or technical problems can prevent execution.

That makes the fixed-position estimate sensitive to both borrower responses and whether intended top-ups actually complete.

Once a position becomes eligible, an external liquidator can repay some or all of its debt and receive collateral plus an incentive. In this market, that exchanges USDC debt repayment for cbXRP collateral.

The exposed positions hold 12.7 million cbXRP, but the amount actually seized depends on subsequent liquidation transactions.

A liquidator may retain or trade that collateral, and any resulting native-XRP sales would require separate evidence linking the liquidation to those sales.

For holders using XRP-backed credit, the immediate pressure is to keep debt supported as collateral values change. Subsequent repayments, collateral additions and executed liquidations will determine how much of this snapshot's exposure remains.

#5181. Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance Cuts Off SignalPlus API Keys Over 'Potential Security Issue' at Trading Platform

Binance has removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus, a third-party crypto trading platform, after SignalPlus reported a "potential security issue," the exchange said on Thursday.

"Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe," the exchange said, describing the move as an "immediate precautionary measure."

Following a potential security issue reported by third-party platform SignalPlus, we have proactively removed all API keys linked to SignalPlus as an immediate precautionary measure.

Binance's own systems are unaffected. User funds remain safe.

If you used SignalPlus, please… pic.twitter.com/0HYYkTAW2p

API keys let third-party apps and trading tools connect to a user's exchange account and place trades on their behalf. Removing them cuts off that connection, meaning any automated strategies running on Binance through SignalPlus will stop until users set up new keys.

Binance urged SignalPlus users to log into their accounts directly to review their positions and open orders. It recommended that they wait until SignalPlus confirms the issue is fully resolved before reconnecting, and that they always enable IP whitelisting, which restricts an API key to approved internet addresses.

The exchange also called on all third-party platforms integrated with Binance to review their security controls, watch for unusual activity and contact it immediately if they spot any concerns.

SignalPlus, a Hong Kong-based platform that provides trading tools for crypto options and derivatives, connects to several exchanges, including Binance.

The incident comes a week after Binance was hit by the largest hack in crypto history, in which attackers drained about $6.4 billion from its hot wallets on October 29. The FBI has attributed the theft to North Korean hackers it calls TraderTraitor, a claim Pyongyang has denied.

Binance has said the attack began when a fake recruiter persuaded one of its engineers to download a malware-laced coding assignment, and that the attackers then used stolen credentials to reach an internal wallet signing service.

Binance said on Wednesday that it has replenished about 98% of the stolen assets from its own funds, and is still buying the rest, mostly less liquid tokens, through market and over-the-counter channels. It has also taken a new proof-of-reserves snapshot covering 44 tokens, up from 10, which users will be able to verify once its zero-knowledge proof is generated in about 50 to 60 hours.

The exchange is still restoring services. On Thursday, it began reopening withdrawals for more assets in phases, prioritizing tokens based on user demand over the past week. Deposits remain open.

#5180. Bitcoin erases post-election gains as Wall Street selloff triggers over $1 billion in crypto liquidations

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

The selloff triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets, as total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached $1.09 billion, affecting 211,000 traders.

Bitcoin, Ethereum, and other cryptocurrencies erased their post-election gains on Thursday as a sharp selloff in U.S. technology and AI stocks spilled into cryptocurrency markets, triggering more than $1 billion in leveraged position liquidations over the past 24 hours.

Bitcoin (BTC) fell 6% to $75,700, after touching an intraday low of $75,568, while ether (ETH) dropped more than 7% to $2,247. The declines reversed Wednesday's rally, when bitcoin climbed back above $80,000 following the U.S. midterm elections and signs that the Binance hack had not caused broader market disruption.

Midterm election results appeared to generate short-term optimism, but analysts say they haven't resolved macroeconomic uncertainties.

As of 2 p.m. ET, the Dow Jones Industrial Average and S&P 500 were down about 3% for the day, while the tech-heavy Nasdaq Composite had dropped 4.7%. The Cboe Volatility Index, or VIX, surged approximately 60%, reflecting heightened investor anxiety.

AI and bitcoin-related mining stocks were among the hardest hit, with Bitdeer and Keel Infrastructure each falling about 20%. American Bitcoin dropped 17% despite announcing a quarterly profit of $126 million. Crypto bellwethers Strategy and Coinbase were each down about 10%.

Of note, the Official Trump (TRUMP) memecoin hit an all-time low after falling more than 20% over the past two days.

The selloff also triggered a wave of forced liquidations across crypto derivatives markets. About $879.92 million in positions were liquidated over a four-hour period, including $818.90 million in longs and $61.02 million in shorts, according to CoinGlass. Total liquidations over the past 24 hours reached nearly $1.1 billion, affecting nearly 211,000 traders.

It should be noted that liquidation figures are typically based on publicly available data, which may significantly understate the extent of market liquidations.

The reversal suggests Wednesday's rebound may have been driven more by temporary optimism than a meaningful improvement in the market's underlying conditions, according to Wintermute.

The trading firm said the rally reflected a combination of relief following the Binance hack, enthusiasm surrounding the election results, optimism about a potential slowdown in AI spending and falling oil prices. However, those factors proved short-lived as broader macroeconomic pressures persisted.

"It's probably a combination of all of them in some way. What matters is that all of them seemed to be so short-lived that it actually didn't really matter," said Jasper De Maere, an OTC trader at Wintermute. "Trump is still the president for two years, the Middle East is still escalating, inflation is elevated into a strong U.S. economy."

Beyond the immediate market pressures, the election results may also complicate the outlook for crypto regulation, potentially tempering some of the optimism that helped drive Wednesday's rebound.

Paul Howard, senior director at Wincent, said the Democrats' strengthened position in Congress could make bipartisan cooperation necessary for meaningful progress on crypto legislation over the next two years. With the Clarity Act's prospects uncertain, Howard expects the existing regulatory framework to largely remain intact until the next election cycle.

Ryan Kirkley, CEO of Global Settlement Network, said the election could delay crypto legislation but wouldn't fundamentally change longer-term institutional demand for digital assets.

"Investors are still focused on liquidity, interest rates and the broader economy, and those factors will continue to influence the market regardless of which party controls Congress," Kirkley said.

If the weakness continues, Wintermute identified $73,000 and $68,000 as the next levels to watch for bitcoin. The firm said it would need to see bitcoin convincingly reclaim and hold $80,000 before turning more positive.

#5179. Rule to Allow Crypto in 401(k)s Arrives at White House for Final Review

A final DOL rule that would ease alternative assets such as private equity, real estate and cryptocurrency into 401(k) retirement plans arrived at the White House on Thursday for review, Bloomberg Law reported, a procedural step that usually precedes public release.

The Office of Information and Regulatory Affairs logged the package on Nov. 5 under the title “Fiduciary Duties In Selecting Designated Investment Alternatives,” RIN 1210-AC38. The filing lists the DOL’s Employee Benefits Security Administration as the authoring office, marks the stage as a final rule, flags it as economically significant and notes that there is no legal deadline for completion.

The DOL unveiled the proposal on March 30. In that draft, plan fiduciaries who follow a documented process when adding private assets to a 401(k) menu would get a legal safe harbor under the Employee Retirement Income Security Act of 1974.

The six factors named in the proposal are performance, fees, liquidity, valuation, performance benchmarks and complexity. Satisfying them does not force plans to offer crypto. It lowers the litigation risk for employers that decide they want to.

JUST IN: 🇺🇸 The White House is now reviewing a new rule which will allow Americans to invest in crypto through their 401(k) retirement plans 👀 pic.twitter.com/uXGGIXEzdC

President Donald Trump’s Aug. 7, 2025, executive order, “Democratizing Access to Alternative Assets for 401(k) Investors,” directed the Labor secretary to reexamine ERISA guidance and, where appropriate, propose rules with “appropriately calibrated safe harbors.” The order defined alternative assets to include private-market equity and debt, real estate, holdings in actively managed vehicles that invest in digital assets, commodities, infrastructure and lifetime-income strategies.

The March proposal implements that order. A DOL fact sheet said prudence under ERISA is process-based and that fiduciaries retain discretion to select any designated investment alternative if they follow the outlined review. Deputy Labor Secretary Keith Sonderling cast the approach as neutral: “The department’s days of picking winners and losers are over.”

That stance reverses a prior chill. In May 2025 the DOL rescinded its March 2022 compliance release that told fiduciaries to use “extreme care” before putting cryptocurrency on a 401(k) menu. The department said that phrase was not found in ERISA and restored a context-specific standard.

Later, it was reported that Sens. Bernie Sanders and Elizabeth Warren pressed the DOL to abandon the crypto-friendly path. House Republicans have separately pushed to codify Trump’s crypto order in statute.

OIRA review under Executive Order 12866 is the last interagency checkpoint before a final rule can be published in the Federal Register. Bloomberg Law said the arrival signals the package is nearing completion. The same office cleared the proposed version in late March before the DOL published it; that earlier pass took roughly two months after the January submission, per prior Bloomberg coverage of the roughly $12 trillion 401(k) market.

What is not yet public is the final text itself. Whether the six-factor safe harbor survived comment intact, how digital-asset vehicles are described, and what effective date the DOL chose will only be clear when OIRA returns the rule and the department posts it. Comment volume on the proposal was heavy; industry and labor groups have argued opposite sides of the prudence standard for months.

For bitcoin, the stakes sit in plan menus rather than spot ETF flows. Trump’s August order helped frame digital assets as a retirement-access issue for more than 90 million defined-contribution savers. A finalized DOL safe harbor would not put bitcoin and crypto on every lineup overnight. It would give plan committees a clearer map if they want exposure through an asset-allocation sleeve that holds digital assets.

The test now is how fast OIRA clears the economically significant package — and whether the published final rule still gives fiduciaries the process cover the March draft promised, or whether the comment fight rewrote the safe harbor before the White House stamps it.

#5178. Stablecoin contraction forces Bitcoin drop despite Fed reserve gains

The dated comparison pairs a reserve rebound with shrinking dollar-token circulation, leaving the link to Bitcoin buying unestablished.

Bank reserve balances rose $67.5 billion between Oct. 28 and Nov. 4, according to the Federal Reserve’s Nov. 5 release, but the more abundant dollar liquidity is not automatically bullish for Bitcoin.

Over the same period, data provider DefiLlama tracked dollar-pegged stablecoin circulation falling by more than 5.7 billion units, or 1.91%.

The split leaves an incomplete picture for a Bitcoin recovery. Higher bank reserve balances would still need to translate into financing and purchases, so the measured token contraction adds a crypto-side observation to that test.

A Nov. 5 Treasury-cash analysis considered a possible December funding window using projected cash paths and the older Oct. 28 reserve baseline. The new release updates that starting point with a rebound in realized reserves.

Coinbase's completed Nov. 5 daily BTC-USD candle closed at $75,969.06, down 5.54% from $80,428.31 on Nov. 4. That close-to-close fall followed the dated reserve observation, putting the price move outside the bank-balance comparison window.

The Fed’s H.4.1 release puts Nov. 4 reserve balances at $2.96 trillion. Over the preceding week, the Treasury General Account, the government’s account at the Fed, fell $79.4 billion to $936.2 billion.

Treasury payments move balances into depository institutions’ accounts, increasing reserves all else equal, while receipts flowing into Treasury’s account work in the opposite direction.

The final reserve change also incorporates movements elsewhere on the Fed’s balance sheet. Total assets rose $2.5 billion, while other balance-sheet changes offset part of the effect of the Treasury-account drawdown.

Tether’s USDT series fell 3.65 billion units, accounting for 63.37% of that measured contraction, while USD Coin (USDC) circulation declined 383 million units, contributing 6.64%. Other tracked dollar-pegged tokens together contracted a net 1.73 billion units.

The issuer breakdown locates the largest measured change in USDT. Whether, and to what extent, issuer redemptions, bridge-accounting adjustments, reclassification, or coverage revisions contributed remains unquantified.

Reserves are banks’ settlement balances at the Fed, while stablecoin circulation measures the tracked stock of dollar-pegged tokens.

DefiLlama’s selected dates carry midnight UTC labels, while the Fed reports Wednesday levels. The comparison aligns calendar labels while leaving the underlying collection times unsynchronized.

For a liquidity-led recovery thesis, further evidence needs to connect reserve balances with financing and buying. A broader token supply could add evidence about crypto-native dollar availability, while proof of Bitcoin demand would still require its own observations.

Neither supply series resolves the cause of the subsequent selloff, and delayed liquidity effects remain possible. The observed divergence leaves the bank-to-Bitcoin demand channel open.

#5177. Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Stablecoins Go Mainstream as Samsung Wallet Adds USDC on Solana

Samsung is putting digital dollars in the same app where its users keep their credit cards and boarding passes.

Stablecoin accounts went live in Samsung Wallet on Thursday for eligible U.S. Galaxy users, letting them buy, hold, send, and receive Circle’s USDC on the Solana blockchain without downloading a separate crypto app or managing private keys. The feature reaches about 82 million compatible Galaxy devices in the U.S., according to the announcement.

“For a decade, stablecoins have proved the technology works,” said Lily Liu, president of the Solana Foundation. “What they lacked was a way to reach the mainstream.”

Samsung first announced the feature on Oct. 7, when the electronics giant tapped Solana for USDC transfers. Samsung Wallet first teased plans to support the stablecoin, which is pegged to the U.S. dollar, over the summer.

The product is built with Bastion, a licensed stablecoin custodian and infrastructure provider, and Coinbase, which safeguards the USDC as Bastion’s sub-custodian through its Coinbase Prime Vault. Samsung’s disclosures stress that the account is a crypto wallet, not a bank account.

Users can send USDC to other Samsung Wallet users in the U.S. and to compatible outside crypto wallets with no Samsung transfer fee, though other providers may charge one. They can also send money to qualifying bank accounts in more than 60 countries, where recipients may receive local currency, with fees that vary by destination.

Topping up with a credit card, including through Samsung Pay, carries a 4% fee, and withdrawals to a bank account or debit card also come with a fee. New users who make a first top-up of $50 or more can receive $25 in USDC. The service requires Android 13 or later and is open to U.S. residents 18 and older, except those in New York.

"Stablecoins have the potential to make moving money around the world faster and easier, and we want Galaxy users to be able to take advantage of that without having to navigate the complexity of traditional crypto tools,” said Woncheol Chai, who leads the digital wallet team in Samsung’s mobile business. “Solana helps us bring that experience to Samsung's scale."

Samsung said it expects to explore online and in-store stablecoin payments and to expand to additional markets, subject to local regulation.

#5174. MEXC sees $126 million outflows as US targets exchange over $16.7 billion in suspected illicit transactions

MEXC says trading remains normal and customer assets are secure, but the Treasury's latest action could complicate its banking relationships.

Crypto exchange MEXC saw $126 million in net outflows as US regulators moved to sever its access to the American financial system.

The withdrawals coincided with a Nov. 6 enforcement action by the Treasury's Financial Crimes Enforcement Network (FinCEN), which accused the exchange of facilitating $16.7 billion in transactions linked to sanctions evasion, terrorist financing and cybercrime.

Data from DeFiLlama showed $126.03 million in net outflows over a 24-hour period spanning the announcement, while the exchange retained approximately $4.353 billion in tracked assets.

This represent net movements through monitored wallets and do not establish that all withdrawals occurred after the announcement or indicate whether the exchange has sufficient assets to meet its customer liabilities.

MEXC has moved to contain concerns about its financial position, insisting that customer funds remain secure and its trading, deposit and withdrawal services are operating normally.

In an official statement, the exchange said it was reviewing the Treasury order with its legal and compliance teams and had initiated an independent examination of its anti-money laundering and sanctions controls.

“We take the concerns raised seriously and intend to engage constructively with FinCEN, as well as other relevant regulators and competent authorities worldwide.”

CEO Vugar Usi separately sought to clarify the implications of the order after concerns spread among users.

“A number of misconceptions are floating around,” Usi said, emphasizing that the restrictions apply to transactions involving US financial institutions rather than the exchange's entire global operation.

“The order itself does not freeze or seize any assets held on MEXC. Users can trade normally.”

Usi expressed confidence that the matter would be resolved and said the exchange would continue updating customers as discussions with regulators progress.

The response followed a sweeping investigation that identified significant deficiencies in MEXC's compliance controls despite its stated policies against servicing customers in sanctioned jurisdictions.

In its 24-page order, FinCEN said MEXC facilitated approximately $16.7 billion in likely illicit activity between May 2021 and May 2026, including transactions involving Russian sanctions evasion networks and Iranian-linked entities.

Nearly $1.5 billion involved direct or indirect exposure to Russian sanctions evasion, including $232 million in direct dealings with sanctioned entities.

FinCEN specifically identified approximately $129 million in transactions involving Garantex and another $4.1 million involving Grinex after the Russian exchanges were sanctioned by Western authorities.

The agency said MEXC continued transacting with both exchanges as recently as June 2026.

Investigators also identified more than $1.87 billion in transactions with Iranian exposure, including approximately $1 billion in direct transfers involving more than 40 Iranian crypto exchanges.

Another $22 million involved Iran's central bank and the Islamic Revolutionary Guard Corps, including its Qods Force.

Separately, the agency identified at least $1.99 billion involving illicit cyber actors, including more than $37.9 million in suspected ransomware payments.

Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent framed the enforcement action as part of Washington's broader effort to restrict financial channels used by sanctioned actors. He said:

“Either you support the Iranian regime, or you have access to the U.S. financial system.”

The order prohibits covered US financial institutions from sending or receiving funds involving MEXC, including cryptocurrency transfers, beginning 30 days after its publication in the Federal Register.

It has no scheduled expiration date, although FinCEN retains the authority to grant exemptions.

The restriction does not shut down MEXC internationally or automatically freeze customer assets, but it could complicate the exchange's relationships with banks, financial intermediaries and liquidity providers that operate within the US regulatory system.

The US enforcement action comes as MEXC retreats from several international markets amid mounting regulatory scrutiny, even as its trading business continues to expand.

As CryptoSlate reported on Oct. 30, the exchange has announced withdrawals from France, the Netherlands, Russia and Belarus as part of its efforts to address compliance concerns.

The latest US intervention adds to those setbacks, potentially complicating MEXC's ambitions to expand beyond cryptocurrency trading into traditional financial markets.

The regulatory pressure comes despite the exchange's growing market presence.

A TokenInsight report showed that MEXC processed $1.069 trillion in trading volume during the third quarter of 2026, ranking fourth among exchanges covered by the study.

Its overall market share reached 8.91%, up 0.38 percentage points from the previous quarter.

MEXC has also gained ground in derivatives linked to traditional financial assets, processing $86 billion in commodity perpetual futures during the quarter. That gave it a 17.43% market share, second only to Binance.

The figures highlight the scale of the trading business now exposed to heightened regulatory uncertainty as MEXC attempts to broaden its presence in global financial markets.

Although FinCEN's order does not prohibit MEXC from operating internationally, it could disrupt relationships with banks, liquidity providers and other intermediaries subject to US jurisdiction.

The extent of that disruption remains unclear because MEXC has not disclosed how much of its trading activity or liquidity depends on institutions covered by the prohibition.

The immediate test will come when the restrictions take effect, particularly whether the exchange can maintain its existing financial relationships while addressing the compliance deficiencies identified by US authorities.

#5171. Strive (ASST) Posts $474.8 Million Common Net Income as Bitcoin Yield Hits 63.2%

Strive reported $474.8 million of net income attributable to common stockholders for the third quarter ended Sept. 30, a print that arrived Friday alongside a company claim that Class A total return had outperformed bitcoin by 103.7% year to date through the same date.

GAAP net income was $509.0 million, or $5.24 a share on a fully diluted basis. Almost all of that came from a $533.7 million unrealized gain on digital assets marked to fair value. Operating revenue for the quarter was just $2.7 million, so the earnings line is a treasury mark, not a fee business.

In a post on X that led with the outperformance figure, Strive also highlighted a year-to-date bitcoin yield of 63.2% and a bitcoin dollar gain of $9.00 per assumed diluted share outstanding, both measured through Sept. 30. Chairman and CEO Matthew Cole tied those metrics to the stock’s relative showing against the asset.

“Strive has continued to successfully execute on its treasury management strategy, delivering a strong bitcoin yield year to date in 2026, which I believe is the primary driver of our common equity’s outperformance relative to Bitcoin so far this year,” Cole said in the earnings release.

Bitcoin yield, as Strive defines it, is the percentage change in bitcoin per share over a period — not a cash yield. On that measure the company posted 18.5% in the third quarter alone and 19.3% from Oct. 1 through Oct. 30, after the SATS preferred debut.

Strive acquired 8,136 bitcoin in the third quarter at an average cost of about $78,885 and 20,373 bitcoin over the nine months ended Sept. 30. Another 15,124 coins followed between Oct. 1 and Oct. 30. Holdings stood at 28,000 bitcoin on Sept. 30 and 43,124 by Oct. 30, keeping Strive among the largest public corporate stacks after recent multi-thousand-coin buys and smaller weekly adds that still trailed Strategy.

Digital assets at fair value were $2.34 billion on the Sept. 30 balance sheet. Cash had risen to $490.4 million by Oct. 30, and Strive still held Strategy’s variable-rate STRC preferred with a fair value of about $50.1 million.

The capital stack that funded the buys is mostly perpetual preferreds. On Oct. 20 Strive closed a registered offering of 7 million shares of its 15% Series A perpetual preferred (SATS) for about $609 million in net proceeds. Preferred dividends of $34.2 million in the quarter are what turned the $509.0 million GAAP profit into the $474.8 million common figure.

Strip the story to nine-month GAAP and the picture flips. For the nine months ended Sept. 30, Strive posted a net loss attributable to common stockholders of $88.4 million. The third-quarter profit is real under fair-value accounting, but it is concentrated in one mark-to-market line.

ASST itself has not moved in a straight line with the company’s bitcoin-yield scoreboard. Shares closed at $20.59 on Thursday, down about 10% on the day, and traded near $20.70 early Friday. That leaves the stock roughly 29% below its level a year earlier, when Yahoo Finance data put the close near $29, and about 33% under its Oct. 21 local high of $30.75.

The 52-week range on Yahoo Finance runs from $7.02 to $34.40. Year to date, the price path from the Jan. 2 close near $17.30 into the mid-$20s is a gain of roughly 20%, with a February low near the 52-week floor and a September push back toward $30. Through Sept. 30 — the cutoff on Strive’s outperformance claim — ASST had risen about 70% from that January close while bitcoin was down roughly 7% over the same window on Yahoo Finance data, a wide gap even before the company’s own total-return methodology.

The longer chart still carries the scar of 2025, when Asset Entities became the public shell for Strive’s bitcoin-treasury reverse merger and the share price spiked into triple digits on weekly closes before collapsing. Investors who held through that conversion saw a different ride than the tidy year-to-date relative-return figure Strive put at the top of Friday’s note.

Cole said the recent SATS issuance leaves Strive “never been in a better position to generate long-term returns in excess of Bitcoin for our common equity shareholders.” The test for ASST from here is whether another quarter of bitcoin yield and preferred-funded buys can reopen the gap to bitcoin after a year in which the stock still sits well below where it traded twelve months ago, even as the treasury has climbed past 30,000 then 40,000 bitcoin.

#5170. Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute: Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Clarity Act Resurfaces

Morning Minute is a daily newsletter written by Tyler Warner. The analysis and opinions expressed are his own and do not necessarily reflect those of Decrypt. And check out our daily news show 'FOMO HOUR' covering all of the top stories and market action.

🗳️ Democrat Sweep Pumps Crypto as Market Structure Bill Resurfaces

Bitcoin spent Tuesday night under $76,000, a seven-week low, with traders bracing for whatever a Democratic Congress meant for crypto. By Wednesday afternoon it was back above $80,800, up 4% on the day.

The easy story is that uncertainty lifted and crypto exhaled. And that could be part of it. But oil helped. WTI fell 9% to $88 a barrel, down almost 17% on the week, as Iran’s parliament speaker said voters had “said NO TO WAR.” The DOW jumped 3% on the day and Nasdaq +1.6% in what was a huge green day across markets.

James Butterfill of CoinShares put it plainly: “For Bitcoin, real yields, dollar liquidity and ETP flows still carry more weight than the elections.”

But the policy news that landed Wednesday afternoon matters more than the candles.

Eleanor Terrett reported that House Democrats are discussing expanding Maxine Waters’ Crypto Investor and Consumer Bill of Rights, originally an amendment to the CLARITY Act in 2025, into a full market structure bill next Congress. The draft would fold in Democratic CLARITY provisions that the House rejected last year, plus provisions Senate Democrats and Republicans had already agreed on before the September cloture vote that killed the bill. Three industry sources described the talks to her.

That’s a genuine surprise. Twenty-four hours ago the consensus was that market structure legislation was dead for two years. Waters is the likely incoming chair of House Financial Services, and she is apparently planning to write a bill rather than hold hearings. But the bill will have some changes.

The new proposals under discussion would give the SEC a larger role supervising crypto markets and leave the CFTC with a smaller one. The draft is expected to carry ethics provisions restricting the crypto activities of Trump and other federal officials. Those provisions are what Democrats wanted all along, and what many thought would kill any chances the bill has of getting signed.

So one of the first courses of action from this Democrat-led Congress is to enact some reasonable changes to the Clarity Act to try to get it passed in 2027. That feels like a very bullish first step.

#5169. Wall Street can stop a tokenized share, but can it stop a stock perp?

Wall Street is fighting over crypto-style stock trading as major companies reject tokenized shares and traditional exchanges challenge new derivatives markets.

Costco recently made news when it strongly objected to a blockchain version of its shares. And while the supermarket giant did raise a set of pretty valid concerns regarding the potential risks of tokenization, the problem was that traders can actually already buy a blockchain-based contract (i.e., token) that follows its stock price.

The company is among 67 issuers in OKXICE's Nov. 5 objection notice, blocking third-party tokenized versions of their stock from the proposed venue backed by the New York Stock Exchange's owner.

Meanwhile, Kalshi lists a Costco perpetual, which offers price exposure without giving its buyer any shares.

The two products show how differently finance treats owning a company and trading its price: an issuer can stop its stock tokens from trading through one blockchain arrangement, while contracts tied to that stock follow a separate legal route.

That route is now being challenged in court, with established Wall Street firms competing to offer these contracts.

Shares give shareholders an interest in a business, along with the voting and distribution rights attached to the particular class of stock they own. Putting that interest on a blockchain can change how it's transferred while preserving what its owner is entitled to receive.

OKXICE's proposed model uses a tokenization provider acting through a registered broker-dealer to hold one underlying share for each token. Each token represents an entitlement to that share, and customers would trade them against dollar-linked stablecoins in automated asset pools.

Those pools can produce different prices from conventional exchanges. The ownership claim and the price you can get for it are separate concerns.

The Securities and Exchange Commission's September exemption allows qualifying venues to operate outside ordinary exchange registration, subject to conditions.

Tokens must preserve equivalent shareholder rights, including votes, dividends, and a claim on residual assets if the company is liquidated. Any token that just copies a share price doesn't qualify.

But the underlying company also gets a say. Before offering stock tokenized by an unaffiliated third party, an exchange has to notify the issuer, and each company has 30 days from receiving its notice to object.

The SEC's staff guidance confirms that a timely objection must be disclosed and prevents the venue from offering that token under the exemption.

Costco and dozens of others have already exercised that right, with their objections applying to OKXICE, and the companies haven't collectively explained their motives. The list therefore tells us little about their views on other forms of tokenization or contracts referencing their share prices.

A Costco perp works differently. You go long to gain when the stock rises, or short to gain when it falls. The contract has no scheduled expiry, and Kalshi's stock-perps documentation says holders receive neither shares nor voting rights.

Reported trailing 24-hour notional volume from one Kalshi data snapshot retrieved Nov. 6, 2026, at 13:49 UTC, rounded to the nearest $1,000. Covers only the four stocks named in Kalshi's Nov. 3 launch announcement, not its full stock-perp market. The exact rolling-window cutoff is not separately timestamped. Turnover is not investor count, new capital, or a measure of bullish demand.

Source: Kalshi market data, retrieved Nov. 6, 2026, 13:49 UTC · Kalshi's Nov. 3 stock-perpetual launch announcement

That also means you can take substantial exposure with relatively little money. Costco's market page showed maximum leverage of 4.9 times on Nov. 6, meaning $1,000 of collateral supports $4,900 of price exposure.

A 10% move would cost $490 before fees and other adjustments, nearly half the original collateral. If collateral falls below the required minimum, the platform can close the position.

Even though the contract has no expiry, you may lose the ability to wait for a recovery.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

Hypothetical arithmetic, not observed returns or a forecast. Start with $1,000: an unlevered stock purchase has $1,000 exposure; a 4.9x long perp has $4,900. Assumes identical percentage price moves and unchanged position size. Loss = starting exposure x price decline. Excludes fees, funding, dividends, lot-size rounding and other adjustments. Liquidation could occur before an illustrated decline, so the bars are not a path an actual position can necessarily survive. The 4.9x input is Costco's displayed maximum on Nov. 6, 2026.

Source: Kalshi Costco market: displayed maximum leverage on Nov. 6, 2026 · Kalshi stock-perp mechanics and liquidation risks; illustrative calculations by CryptoSlate

Perps also need a way to keep their price near the underlying asset. Conventional futures have an eventual settlement date, while perpetuals use recurring payments between traders, called funding, to discourage a persistent gap.

When the contract trades too high relative to its reference price, longs have to pay shorts, and when it trades too low, payments run the other way.

These funding transfers can reward taking the less popular side, but they also mean that correctly predicting a stock's direction doesn't fully determine your return.

Kalshi says cash dividends generate contractual cash adjustments for long and short positions, although those payments don't make contract holders shareholders, nor does perpetual mean permanently open.

Kalshi's Costco page lists trading from Sunday evening to Friday afternoon, Eastern time. Its documentation warns that customers cannot open or close positions during closures.

On Nov. 3, CFTC Chairman Michael Selig said equity perpetuals could begin trading on eligible exchanges registered with the derivatives regulator and notice-registered with the SEC. He described the products as deemed approved under Regulation 40.3, which permits approval when the review period expires without a notice of non-approval.

The agency treats them as security futures, placing them under a joint regulatory framework. Their lack of an expiry date is at the heart of the challenge to that classification.

The Capital Markets Alliance Association, whose court filings include declarations from Cboe and Citadel Securities, wants to stop the approvals. It argues that a contract without future delivery cannot qualify as a security future.

In an October SEC submission, Cboe argued that the contracts fall under security-based swaps, agreements that transfer financial exposure linked to securities under different rules. It also warned that stock-perp trades would escape the securities markets' consolidated surveillance system even when traders hedge them with ordinary shares.

Cboe has a commercial stake in the outcome, but its argument raises a substantive issue about which safeguards should follow a trade that tracks a stock without delivering it.

Judge Valerie Caproni's Nov. 3 order found the challengers likely to succeed in arguing that the CFTC exceeded its authority. She nevertheless denied an emergency restraining order because the alliance hadn't demonstrated the imminent, irreparable harm required for that relief.

The approvals remained undisturbed at that stage, despite the judge's preliminary assessment of the CFTC's legal position. The order set Nov. 6 for the government's opposition to a preliminary injunction and Nov. 10 for the alliance's reply.

Meanwhile, the NYSE's parent is pursuing both markets. ICE announced an investment and agreement with Trade[XYZ] on Oct. 29 to bring its Hyperliquid-based perpetual markets into the US.

The planned regulated venue still requires approvals, including for leveraged trading. ICE expects distribution to include its OKX joint venture, subject to that venture's own approvals.

The same exchange group backing tokenized shares sees an opportunity in selling exposure to their prices. The dispute is partly over which institutions can offer crypto's trading model, and which rules they must follow.

The difference for investors is what each product promises. Qualifying share tokens must preserve shareholder rights while introducing the costs and risks of their trading platform. Perps offer leveraged exposure governed by collateral requirements, funding payments, and contractual adjustments.

Costco's objection shows how an issuer can stop the first product on a particular venue, while the court challenge concerns regulators' authority to approve the second. The ticker may be familiar, but the rights and risks come from the contract you buy.

Armed with a classical education and an eye for news, Andjela dove head deep into the crypto industry in 2018 after spending years covering politics.

Gino Matos is a law school graduate and a seasoned journalist with six years of experience in the crypto industry. His expertise primarily focuses on the Brazilian blockchain…

#5167. IREN’s bitcoin mining revenue drops 58% as firm plans full exit by year-end

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

IREN has been shifting its focus to the AI infrastructure business, and its AI cloud revenue rose 182% in the past quarter to $200 million.

IREN (IREN) reported bitcoin Bitcoin (BTC) mining revenue of $28 million in the three months ended Sept. 30, which is a 58% quarter-over-quarter decline from $67 million and an 88% drop from $233 million a year earlier.

According to the company's 10-Q filing, IREN mined 402 BTC during the period, compared with 2,039 BTC in the same quarter last year. Average operating hashrate fell to 8.8 EH/s from 45.3 EH/s year-over-year. IREN held no bitcoin on its balance sheet as of the end of September as it liquidates mined bitcoin daily.

"We continue to operate Bitcoin miners at certain of our data centers while we transition this data center capacity toward AI Cloud," IREN wrote in the filing. "We aim to substantially complete this transition by December 31, 2026, with immaterial Bitcoin mining operations expected to continue through calendar year 2027."

IREN, which launched in 2018, announced in March last year that it will stop expanding its bitcoin mining operations and shift its focus to AI and high-performance computing through AI cloud services and data center businesses. Competing bitcoin miner firms MARA and CleanSpark have also turned to the AI infrastructure business.

Consequently, the company's quarterly revenue rose 66% sequentially to $228 million, driven by AI cloud revenue of around $200 million, which is up 182% from the prior quarter. The company posted a net loss of $263 million, narrower than the $684 million loss in the previous quarter, while adjusted EBITDA rose to $66 million from $19 million.

IREN said it has $20.6 billion in total assets, including $6 billion of data center assets, with assets and revenue underpinned by customer contracts with Nvidia, Microsoft, and 13 other customers.

The firm's Nasdaq-listed shares fell 17% on Thursday to close at $18.03, in line with a broader sell-off across AI and tech stocks.

Meanwhile, analysts at Bernstein gave IREN an Outperform rating on Friday, raising the price target to $55. They cited the firm’s “self-owned, unencumbered” physical power assets and the flexibility to decide its own pace of development.

#5165. Bitcoin traders could mistake this Binance data change for a rush to sell

Coin Metrics’ November 4 notice complicates Binance balance signals as traders look for evidence of demand before CPI.

Bitcoin is trading near $76,000 ahead of Tuesday's US inflation report, while reported spot selling puts the strength of demand in focus. A warning from data provider Coin Metrics complicates one apparent signal: a larger reported Binance wallet balance can reflect expanded address coverage without fresh coins arriving.

Bitcoin was quoted at $75,871 on CryptoSlate's price page when captured at 10:42 UTC on Nov. 7. That puts $76,000 in view as a rounded market price, rather than an established technical support level.

CryptoSlate's Nov. 7 report cited CryptoQuant analyst Darkfost describing more aggressive spot selling than buying on Binance and Coinbase. Coin Metrics' coverage notice concerns a different measurement and does not invalidate that trading signal.

The upcoming inflation release gives traders a defined window to compare potential selling with actual demand. A balance chart alone cannot show whether holders have just moved coins onto an exchange, or sold them.

In its Nov. 4 notice, Coin Metrics said it planned to update exchange-address data after additional addresses were disclosed. It anticipated significant increases in some hourly supply metrics that day and daily metrics on Nov. 5, particularly for Binance BTC measures in native units and US dollars.

The provider said the planned changes would reflect broader address coverage without a corresponding change in actual exchange holdings. The notice supplies a reason to question an apparent jump, but it does not quantify one.

Coin Metrics' exchange-supply methodology counts balances in identified hot and cold wallets at the end of an interval. It also warns that undiscovered addresses can leave the measure below actual holdings. Identifying an additional wallet therefore changes what the dataset sees, without establishing that a customer has just deposited more Bitcoin.

Dollar-denominated supply adds another moving part: it is calculated from the coin balance and the USD price. A rise in that dollar reading alone cannot isolate an increase in the number of coins.

The next step is to examine deposits, which Coin Metrics defines as assets sent to an exchange during an interval, using addresses it identifies as exchange-owned. Those observations measure transfers. They do not establish that the transferred coins were sold.

Comparability matters here too. Coin Metrics' point-in-time documentation says standard exchange histories can be revised when new addresses are discovered. Point-in-time histories keep past values fixed, but newly discovered addresses still enter coverage from their discovery date onward. Choosing that series does not automatically make every date comparable.

Evidence of execution comes from a different dataset. Coin Metrics' trade records include time, price and amount, plus the taker's side where known. A sell taker is an incoming order that removes a resting bid. That identifies aggressive selling, rather than merely coins held at an exchange.

A sell-taker trade matches an incoming sell order with a standing buy order, where the taker's side is known.

Expanded wallet coverage can raise reported supply without a holdings change; deposits and sales require different observations.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics calendar schedules October CPI for Tuesday, Nov. 10 at 8:30 a.m. Eastern Time, or 13:30 UTC.

Inflation news could change expectations for monetary policy, with Treasury yields and appetite for risk among the possible transmission channels. The Federal Reserve's explanation describes how expected policy changes can affect longer-term rates and asset prices. These are possible channels for the coming release, rather than a prediction of Bitcoin's direction.

For this demand test, comparable flows would show whether coins are moving onto exchanges, while executed spot trades would show whether sellers are executing against standing buy orders. The price response would then help assess whether buyers are absorbing that pressure: aggressive selling alongside a resilient price would be consistent with absorption; sustained price weakness would make that reading harder to defend.

These observations need to cover comparable periods and measurements. They would not, by themselves, connect a particular depositor with a particular sale. For holders watching the inflation release, the useful signal is how actual selling meets demand, with Binance's changing wallet coverage accounted for first.

#5164. Former FBI Agent Pleads Guilty to Stealing $2.47 Million in Crypto From Investigations

A former FBI agent pleaded guilty Thursday to stealing about $2.47 million in cryptocurrency with wallet credentials the bureau had collected in its own cases, the U.S. Attorney’s Office for the Eastern District of Virginia announced.

Patrick Steven Yaroch, 37, of Ashburn, Virginia, entered pleas to wire fraud and money laundering in federal court in Alexandria. U.S. District Judge Patricia Tolliver Giles accepted the plea. He faces up to 20 years in prison and is scheduled for sentencing on March 4, 2027.

Court papers put the illicit haul at $2,474,380.66. Prosecutors say Yaroch made roughly 100 fraudulent transfers using credentials tied to 35 separate FBI investigations, drawing on wallets controlled by investigation subjects, adversarial nation-state actors and at least one wallet the FBI itself had created for an operation.

The Justice Department Office of the Inspector General had flagged the case in August after Yaroch’s arrest on a criminal complaint. That earlier account described a smaller set of transfers — about 10 moves totaling roughly $1 million — and noted that Yaroch self-reported the activity to two department employees on July 29, 2026. The plea figures are larger.

From 2017 through early 2025, Yaroch worked in the FBI Boston Division on a squad focused on an adversarial nation-state. From February 2025 through July 31, 2026, he served in the FBI’s Counterintelligence and Espionage Division at headquarters, with access to systems that stored cryptocurrency credentials gathered in investigations.

Beginning no later than December 2024, Yaroch used that access to move coins out of those wallets and into ones he controlled. After the transfers, he swapped the stolen cryptocurrency into other digital assets and ran further transactions meant to obscure its source, nature and ownership.

Between Nov. 25, 2025, and Jan. 13, 2026, he moved about $93,082 into one of his accounts, then shuffled funds among other accounts to cover debts, including credit cards, car payments and loans, the office said.

FOX 5 DC, citing the same U.S. Attorney’s Office release, noted the Portugal planning that appears in the plea papers. Yaroch asked ChatGPT how to maximize investment returns and where to live in the European Union. After the chatbot suggested buying a vineyard in Portugal, he gave Portuguese lawyers a power of attorney on June 15, 2026, to register him with the Portuguese Tax and Customs Authority, obtain a tax identification number and collect the password to his finance portal. He arranged travel to Portugal for September 2026.

The agreed statement of facts goes beyond the dollar figure. Yaroch’s conduct compromised multiple FBI investigations into criminal groups and nation-state adversaries, the office said, and it compromised FBI sources and methods used in foreign intelligence collection and criminal cases. It also “deprived FBI employees of their ability to carry out their functions to protect public safety and national security.”

First Assistant U.S. Attorney Theophani K. Stamos and Daniel S. Wierzbicki, special agent in charge of the FBI Washington Field Office’s Counterintelligence Division, announced the plea. Assistant U.S. Attorneys Kathleen Robeson and Russell Carlberg are prosecuting, with help from the National Security Cyber Section of the Justice Department’s National Security Division and the DOJ inspector general’s Mid-Atlantic region. The case is 1:26-CR-208 in the Eastern District of Virginia.

The admission lands in a week when federal crypto-crime stories keep stacking up — from a failed bitcoin kidnapping plot that produced its own guilty plea last month to FBI tallies putting U.S. crypto fraud losses above $11 billion in 2025, a figure the White House has used to frame a wider crackdown.

What sentencing will do with the gap between Yaroch’s July self-report and the $2.47 million plea figure is still open. So is how many of the 35 FBI cases can be repaired after an insider treated the bureau’s own wallet credentials as a personal ATM. March 4, 2027, is when Judge Giles gets to answer the first question.

#5162. Zcash’s major network upgrade NU7 goes live on mainnet, cutting block time by threefold

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

NU7 also activates the Network Sustainability Mechanism, which removes 60% of each block's transaction fees from circulation and stores them in a public reserve.

Zcash Zcash (ZEC) announced that its major network upgrade, NU7, activated on the mainnet at block height 3,543,405 on Nov. 5.

The upgrade reduces the target block interval from 75 seconds to 25 seconds. Block subsidies are scaled down by the same factor so that the rate of new ZEC issuance remains essentially unchanged. The number of blocks between halvings is tripled, which preserves the existing halving schedule and the 21 million ZEC supply cap.

NU7 also activates the Network Sustainability Mechanism. The mechanism removes 60% of each block's transaction fees from circulation and stores it in a public reserve, with miners receiving the remaining 40%. The reserved fees are scheduled for gradual reissuance through block rewards beginning at block 8,939,664, expected to start in 2031.

"Like Bitcoin, Zcash faces a long-term challenge: how to sustain network security as block rewards decline. The NSM helps address that while preserving the 21 million coin cap," said Shielded Labs, an independent organization supporting the development of Zcash. The organization said NSM is its first major mainnet deployment on Zcash.

The upgrade also disallows version 4 transactions. As the original Sprout shielded pool relies exclusively on that format, any ZEC remaining in Sprout becomes unspendable. Approximately 12,224 ZEC sat in the pool at the activation height.

Node operators were required to upgrade software before the activation height to remain on the consensus chain. The changes do not require significant updates for most wallets.

ZEC dropped 5.17% in the past 24 hours to trade at $1,254.7 as of 2:05 a.m. on Friday, amid a broader downturn in the crypto market. The GMCI 30 index, measuring the performance of the top 30 cryptocurrencies, is down 6.9%.

#5161. Higher wages don’t have to mean higher inflation. That could matter for Bitcoin

Preliminary Q3 productivity outpaced hourly compensation, offering a cost counterpoint while inflation keeps the Fed cautious.

US nonfarm businesses produced more per hour in the third quarter, allowing labor costs per unit of output to fall even as hourly compensation rose. For Bitcoin, that offers a potential offset to jobs-driven rate fears: if lower cost pressure helps cool inflation and expected interest rates, financing could become less expensive.

The Bureau of Labor Statistics' preliminary November 5 release showed nonfarm business productivity rising 3.6% as output increased 3.7% and hours worked rose 0.1%. Hourly compensation increased 2.9%, while unit labor costs fell 0.7%. All those quarterly changes are seasonally adjusted annualized rates.

Unit labor costs track compensation relative to output. BLS divides hourly compensation by productivity: when each hour produces more, businesses can pay more per hour without incurring higher labor costs for each unit they produce.

Q3 productivity grew faster than hourly compensation. The quarterly cost decline coexisted with a positive year-over-year cost reading.

Productivity can absorb higher pay; quarterly labor-cost relief does not settle the inflation outlook.

Preliminary Q3 nonfarm business estimates, published November 5, 2026. Quarterly figures are seasonally adjusted annualized rates; the 2.4% figure is year over year. This is not consumer-price inflation, October productivity or a Bitcoin response.

Plot reported Q3 annualized changes on one linear scale: 80 SVG units per percentage point on desktop and 44 on mobile. The 2.4% four-quarter change is shown separately without a comparable bar. No growth-rate subtraction or new calculation.

Preliminary Q3 nonfarm business estimates, published November 5, 2026. Quarterly figures are seasonally adjusted annualized rates; the 2.4% figure is year over year. This is not consumer-price inflation, October productivity or a Bitcoin response.

That complicates a reading of the November 6 employment report as an automatic inflation warning. October payrolls increased 165,000, above the prior 12 months' average monthly gain of 48,000. Average hourly earnings for private nonfarm employees rose 0.4% over the month and 3.1% over a year.

Thanks for signing up to The Catalyst. Look out for your first daily email. If you already receive it, you’re all set.

The reports cover different periods and measures. Third-quarter productivity does not establish how productive workers were in October, and its annualized growth rate cannot simply be subtracted from monthly earnings growth. Higher hiring and pay alone leave the question of labor costs per unit of output unanswered.

Nonfarm business unit labor costs remained 2.4% higher than a year earlier. The quarterly decline eases one source of cost pressure, while leaving both the durability of that improvement and its effect on consumer prices unsettled.

The Fed's latest decision illustrates the policy tension. On October 28, before either November labor release, the Federal Open Market Committee held its target range at 3.75% to 4.00% in a 10-2 vote. Lisa Cook and Beth Hammack preferred a quarter-point increase.

That statement described productivity growth as strong while calling inflation elevated. The two conditions already coexisted in the Fed's assessment, limiting the case for treating better productivity as an automatic signal of easier policy.

For Bitcoin, the financing channel depends on what happens to rate expectations. Expected short-term rates can influence longer-term rates, borrowing terms and the relative attractiveness of assets. A sustained decline in inflation pressure could ease a potential rates headwind for Bitcoin if it brings lower expected rates and funding costs.

Persistent inflation or upward labor-cost revisions would weaken the case for relief in Bitcoin prices.

#5160. Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

Sam Altman Says It's 'Fucking Annoying' His RAM Comments Were Taken Out of Context

OpenAI CEO Sam Altman has hit back at criticism of his comments about computer memory, saying they were taken out of context and that he has long argued for the world to make more of it.

"I actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context," Altman tweeted early Wednesday. "I have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time."

but only because...i actually do want the world to have tons of memory and people to put as much as they want in their computers, and it is fucking annoying that this was taken out of context.

i have been arguing for the world to ramp up memory production for a long time.

The post came as Altman leaned into the backlash. Replying to users joking about his remarks, he said he "lol'd" at one meme and, when another asked him to rename his X account "Ram Altman," replied "ok yeah," briefly changing his display name before explaining he had only done so because he wanted people to have plenty of memory.

Altman's original comments came in an interview with YouTube channel Cheeky Pint, where he discussed shortages of memory and other components caused by the AI boom. "From like a global allocation perspective, actually, it's quite terrible to have everybody have a bunch of the world's limited supply of DRAM sitting in their own computer and being used 5% of the time," he said. "You want maximum utilization in a cloud if you're trying to deal with this very supply-constrained environment."

In the same interview, Altman also blamed memory makers for being slow to expand production, and said he would be "very sad" if prices for chips and other components did not fall.

The remarks drew widespread criticism, including from Elon Musk, who mocked Altman on Sunday with a meme of the Inglourious Basterds character Hans Landa asking, "You are withholding DRAM from the cloud, are you not?" "The people deserve to have their RAM too!" Musk wrote.

The spat comes amid a global memory shortage driven by demand from AI data centers. SK hynix, one of the world's largest memory makers, warned last week that demand for memory would outstrip supply through 2030.

Contract prices for 16GB DDR5 memory chips rose from $8.70 in October 2025 to $48 in September 2026, an increase of more than 550%, according to Tom's Hardware. On an earnings call this week, Chen Ching-wen, CEO of memory maker Team Group, said there was "no downward price trend whatsoever," and warned that some buyers could face a situation where "even money can't buy goods."

The crunch has already pushed up prices for consumer devices. In April, Meta raised the price of its Quest 3 and Quest 3S headsets by $50 to $100, weeks after Sony increased PlayStation 5 prices.

#5158. Kalshi hit with injunction over sports contracts on California tribal lands

Get The Block’s latest crypto news and price alerts for the coins you follow, right in Chrome. Add our free extension here.

A federal judge has granted a preliminary injunction barring Kalshi from offering sports-event contracts on the lands of Blue Lake Rancheria and the Chicken Ranch Rancheria of Me-Wuk Indians.

A federal judge has granted a preliminary injunction barring Kalshi from offering sports-event contracts on the lands of two California tribes.

In a Wednesday order, U.S. District Judge Jacqueline Scott Corley ruled that the injunction covers contracts tied to sports including football, basketball, baseball, soccer, tennis, and Ultimate Fighting Championship events.

The tribes — Blue Lake Rancheria and the Chicken Ranch Rancheria of Me-Wuk Indians — must provide boundary maps by Nov. 13, the judge said.

Kalshi is required to report the status of the steps it has taken to comply with the injunction within 21 days, according to the order.

Sports and gaming attorney Daniel Wallach described the order as the first preliminary injunction secured by tribes against Kalshi nationwide, calling it "a major development in tribal gaming litigation."

The tribes filed the lawsuit in July 2025, alleging that Kalshi's sports event contracts constitute unauthorized Class III gaming under the Indian Gaming Regulatory Act (IGRA) and violate the tribes' gaming ordinances.

Kalshi has argued that its sports event contracts are federally regulated swaps under the Commodity Exchange Act (CEA), and that the CEA's grant of exclusive jurisdiction to the Commodity Futures Trading Commission preempts tribal gaming law.

The judge initially denied the tribes' motion for a preliminary injunction in November 2025. The tribes then appealed, and a Ninth Circuit panel partially reversed the decision in September.

"Reversing the district court's denial of a preliminary injunction in part, the panel held that the tribes were likely to succeed in their claims that Kalshi's sports event contracts on tribal lands violated IGRA and the tribes' gaming ordinances," the panel wrote.

The Ninth Circuit panel also held that the CEA's "exclusive" jurisdiction did not extend to Kalshi's sports event contracts.

The Block has reached out to Kalshi for comment.

Kalshi faces mounting legal challenges, with more states taking action against the prediction markets platform.

On Wednesday, Arkansas Attorney General Tim Griffin sued Kalshi for allegedly operating an illegal gambling platform in the state.

"It doesn't take a genius to understand that wagering money on whether the Razorbacks win or lose is gambling," Griffin said, adding that such practice is "squarely prohibited" by Arkansas law.

Last week, Utah also filed criminal charges against Kalshi and two company executives, accusing them of illegally promoting online gambling. Utah has also sued Kalshi and two Susquehanna trading firms for allegedly operating an illegal gambling business.
